# Ambiguity Register

Every open design question that blocks a story from being Ready. Claude raises flags here instead of guessing. Westley answers; the answer becomes a rule in the epic file and the flag closes. Closed flags stay in the table with their answer so the reasoning survives.

Schema: `Q-<story>-<nn>` where `<story>` is the story number the flag blocks, or `E##` when it blocks a whole epic that has no story numbers yet.

| Flag | Epic / story | Question | Candidate answers | Status | Answer |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q-E01-01 | Layer System | What elevation does each layer map to on screen, in world units, and is the mapping fixed per era or per map? | (a) fixed per era in the theme manifest; (b) per map from the generator; (c) fixed globally | closed | (c), fixed globally, always 5 layers. "There should always be 5 layers, regardless of era. Only 3 may have been an idea from when we were just implementing a toy version, but we're not doing that anymore, so every era should be a 5 layer system." Elevation mapping fixed globally per era theme (0–30, 30–100, 100–300 wu). Answered by Westley, 2026-09-12. Amended 2026-10-02: the count of five layers stands; the elevation mapping is no longer fixed globally. On land maps the five height bands are fitted to each map (Westley, verbatim under Q-166-01; E-01 S-200, E-17 S-188). |
| Q-E01-02 | Layer System | When a unit's layer changes, does it visibly move to the new elevation, and over how many ticks? | (a) instant snap; (b) tween over N ticks with the unit unable to fire while moving; (c) tween with no restrictions | closed | (b), confirmed. `bible_combat.md` A-354: a unit transitioning between layers spends a per-unit-type duration (Strike fastest, Siege slowest) unable to attack or be targeted by units on its old layer — a real vulnerability window, not an instant snap or an unrestricted tween. Closed from the Stage 1 audit, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E01-03 | Layer System | What can a unit on layer 2 see on layers 1 and 3, and at what range compared to its own layer? | (a) same range, all layers; (b) reduced range on adjacent layers, none beyond; (c) cone and pulse model from `bible_world.md` with concrete numbers | closed | (c), confirmed with the concrete numbers. `bible_world.md` A-432: passive cross-layer cone detection degrades by distance — adjacent layer ×0.50, two layers away ×0.20, three+ layers away no passive detection at all; vantage nodes double these factors and extend one layer further. A-433 adds an active sensor pulse (reveal all layers in a radius for ~2s, ~20s cooldown) on top of the passive cone. Closed from the Stage 1 audit, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E01-04 | Layer System | Do layers change pathing, damage, or cover? Which of the three, and how? | (a) vision only; (b) vision and damage modifiers; (c) vision, damage, and separate pathing per layer | closed | Nuanced answer, none of the three literally as posed. Layers affect **pathing** (`bible_world.md`'s `layer_accessibility_mask` blocks movement per layer/position) and **vision** (the cone/pulse model above), and indirectly **cover** (an obstacle's height determines which layers it blocks line of sight on, per `bible_combat.md` A-337 / `bible_world.md` A-388's `LAYER_Z_FLOORS` rule). Layers do **not** directly affect damage — that's a separate `elevation` gradient gated by `ELEVATION_DAMAGE_BONUS_ENABLED`, disabled for the space era at launch (`bible_world.md` A-435, `bible_combat.md` A-338). Closed from the Stage 1 audit, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E01-05 | Layer System | What does the player see in the HUD about a selected unit's layer and about enemies on other layers? | (a) layer badge on the unit; (b) badge plus per-layer filter toggle; (c) nothing beyond elevation on screen | closed | (b), confirmed. `bible_combat.md` A-312: a layer-selector strip lets the player switch which layer has their focus. A-299 (deferred pending visual-mechanism research): a per-unit layer-preference indicator; A-313: a layer-color ring under every unit shows current layer at a glance. No dedicated "enemies on other layers" HUD readout beyond the fog/sensor system itself. Closed from the Stage 1 audit, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E01-06 | Layer System | Under fog, how does an enemy on another layer appear compared to one on the same layer? | (a) identical; (b) silhouette only on other layers; (c) hidden unless a pulse is active | closed | (a), mechanism-wise. There's no special silhouette treatment for a different-layer enemy — it's the same hidden/revealed binary as same-layer fog, just evaluated against a shorter (cone-degraded, per Q-E01-03) or reset-to-hidden (pulse-expired, per `bible_world.md` A-433) effective sensor range. Closed from the Stage 1 audit, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E01-07 | Layer System | Is the layer system still "the" strategic defining characteristic, and if so what is the one-sentence player-facing promise it must keep? | Westley to write the sentence | closed | Yes, still the defining characteristic. Promise: a player with an inferior force but understanding of positional tactics (surround, high ground, game-theory positioning) can overcome the opposition — the layer system, high skill ceiling, specific unit knowledge decide outcomes over raw force. Answered live in Stage 1 vision interview, `vision_v2.md` Q6, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E02-01 | Simulation Field Floors | `bible_simulation.md`'s tension-floor paragraph says a floor "prevents tension from dropping below a minimum value," then immediately says the floor is "applied during event resolution but not during drift, so drift can push tension toward the floor but events cannot push it below." Does drift-driven tension loss actually stop exactly at the floor in practice, or can it dip below the floor via drift alone, with only event-caused drops actually blocked? | (a) drift is also clamped to the floor in practice — the second sentence is just explaining which code path owns the clamp, not describing a real gap; (b) drift genuinely can push tension below the floor and this is accepted (a soft floor for drift, hard floor for events); (c) this is a real bug/inconsistency to fix, floor should be hard everywhere | closed | Westley: leave as-is for now (Stage 1 audit A-196, 2026-09-10). Not resolving which reading is correct — verify actual behavior with tests when the simulation migrates to C, not before. |
| Q-E03-01 | Combat — Command Vocabulary | `bible_combat.md`'s Command Vocabulary section states, in the same breath, both "no group-move primitive" / "not a speed-matched group move" (line ~434) and then describes "Formation-preserving group move" (line 436): a multi-unit move command computes each unit's own offset from the group centroid and assigns it an individual destination that preserves the group's shape on arrival. Is the shape-preserving destination assignment meant to coexist with "no group-move" (i.e. "no group-move" only ever meant no *speed*-matching, and per-unit destination-slot assignment was always in scope), or is Formation-preserving group move itself the thing that should be cut/rewritten to match a stricter "every unit just goes to the raw click point, no computed offsets" reading? | (a) keep both as written — "no group-move" refers only to speed (units keep native speed, arrive strung out by speed tier), and destination-slot shape preservation is a separate, intentional feature; (b) cut Formation-preserving group move — every mixed-selection move order sends every unit to the same raw point, no computed per-unit offsets, full agreement with "no group-move primitive" taken literally; (c) something else (e.g. shape preservation only for single-type selections, or only below some spread threshold) | closed | (c) — Halo Wars model. Keep both mechanics, but reverse the speed-matching clause: a selection moves together at its slowest unit's pace by default (not strung-out arrival), and formation-preserving destination assignment is exactly the mechanism that makes uniform-pace cohesion work. Players can deliberately split off their own sub-groups to move independently at a different pace if they want a faster element to detach. Reverses `bible_combat.md` A-346's "no speed-matching" claim; A-347 needed no change. New mechanic recorded as `bible` A-557. Closed by Westley, 2026-09-10. |
| Q-E00-01 | Rewind (macro layer) | Multiplayer is back (vision_v2.md Q1/Q3, reversing the 2026-05-18 pivot). Pre-pivot bible language read "Rewind is a single-player tool. Its behavior under multiplayer is open." That hedge was deleted (not resolved) by the 2026-05-30 cleanup commit. Does chapter-level rewind still work in a multiplayer match, and if so how — host-only, vote-gated, disabled entirely in multiplayer, or something else? | (a) rewind is single-player-only, disabled/unavailable in any multiplayer match; (b) rewind works in multiplayer but only the host/session owner can trigger it; (c) rewind requires all connected players to agree/vote; (d) something else | closed | Rewind tool removed entirely (Stage 1 audit A-027/A-028, 2026-09-10) — it was a single-player-era design and doesn't fit the confirmed multiplayer vision. Question moot. |
| Q-001-01 | E-01 / S-001 | How many layers does a map have? `bible_combat.md` says 3 to 5 per map; code has `LAYER_COUNT = 5` but only three elevation bands (`LAYER_Z_FLOORS`). Is the count fixed engine-wide with maps blocking unused layers, or variable per map? | (a) fixed at 5 everywhere, maps block layers they do not use; (b) per map, 3 to 5, chosen by the generator and carried in the map seed; (c) per era pack | closed | (a), confirmed by Q-E01-01 verdict: always 5 layers engine-wide. Settled Reach does not restrict any layers; all 5 are open at every position. Map generator does not vary layer count per map; the 3-to-5 bible language is stale. Answered by Westley, 2026-09-12. Amended 2026-10-02: this holds on space maps. On land maps the ground fills the layers below its surface (E-01 S-200, E-17 S-190). |
| Q-001-02 | E-01 / S-001 | Which end of the stack is layer 1? `bible_combat.md` says an obstacle "occupying layers 1–2 forces units to maneuver over it on layers 3–5" (1 is lowest) and in the same section says a 1980s pack restricts "submarines to layers 4–5 (underwater)... aircraft to layers 1–2 (airspace)" (1 is highest). Contradiction. | (a) 1 is lowest, 5 is highest, fix the submarine example; (b) 1 is highest, fix the obstacle example; (c) number from the top and rename to avoid the word "layer 1" in player-facing text | closed | (a). "The bottom layer is layer 1 (lowest in terms of height)." Fix submarine example in bible: submarines layers 1–2 (underwater, lowest), aircraft layers 4–5 (airspace, highest). Answered by Westley, 2026-09-12. |
| Q-001-03 | E-01 / S-001 | A unit is ordered to a destination and no route exists on its current layer because a blocked zone cuts the map. Does it stop at the blockage, change layer on its own to get through, or reject the order? | (a) path as far as possible and stop, never auto-change layer (preserves "layer is the player's decision"); (b) auto-transition to the nearest open layer for the blocked stretch; (c) reject the move order with a reason code | closed | Terrain-forced auto-transition. Westley, 2026-09-30, verbatim: "The player owns the layer, so nothing ever changes it automatically." Unless their units are forced because of terrain. If they move-command someplace that doesn't have access to the layer they're on, but does have access on another layer that they can transition to, then their units automatically transition (like units walking up a mountain). Record that decision. Replaces the 2026-09-12 answer except what still stands from it, Westley, 2026-09-12, verbatim: "Units shoudl route around obstacles, just like classic starcraft 2 RTS." A destination blocked on every layer the unit may occupy stops the unit at the blockage. Folded into E-01 R-001-05; the choices the two answers leave open are Q-001-04. |
| Q-001-04 | E-01 / S-001, S-002, S-003 | Westley's 2026-09-30 ruling (Q-001-03) says a unit whose destination is blocked on its layer but open on another auto-transitions. It does not say: (1) whether a detour on the current layer beats a forced change when both reach the destination; (2) where the change happens; (3) how a forced change meets a pending hard transfer (S-002) or a layer preference (S-003); (4) which layer it goes to when several are open. | (a) [recommended] (1) detour on the current layer wins whenever one exists, so the forced change fires only when no path exists on the current layer (the 2026-09-12 "route around obstacles"); (2) at the nearest position from which the target layer is open; (3) the forced change happens and a pending transfer stays pending; (4) the open layer nearest the current one, preference breaking a tie; (b) a forced change is taken whenever it gives the shorter route; (c) other | closed | (b), the shortest route wins. Answered by Westley, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "8: alternative, shortest route wins (if unit is on layer 1 and player move commands to a location on layer 1, but layer 2 is on the shortest path, the unit traverses the layer on the shortest path to get to the goal)." A unit takes the shortest route to its goal across every layer it may use and changes layer on the way where that is shorter; this replaces the detour-first reading. Folded into E-01 R-001-01, R-001-05, R-002-02, R-003-03; what it leaves open is Q-001-05. |
| Q-001-05 | E-01 / S-001, S-002, S-003 | Shortest-route layer changes (Q-001-04) leave three things unsaid: (1) whether "shortest" is by distance or by time, since a timed transfer and a slow climb cost time and no distance; (2) which layer a flying unit ends on when its goal is open on several layers; (3) how a route that changes layer meets a pending hard transfer (S-002) or a layer preference (S-003). | (a) [recommended] (1) by travel time, counting each transfer's duration and each slope's length at the unit's speed; (2) the layer it was on when ordered, as in Westley's example (ordered on layer 1 to a place on layer 1, crossing layer 2 on the way); (3) the route may leave a preferred layer and the unit returns to it where it is open, and a pending hard transfer stays pending until the move completes; (b) (1) by distance alone; (2) whatever layer the route arrives on; (3) a preference or pending transfer forbids routes that leave that layer; (c) other | closed | Provisional. Westley, 2026-10-01, asked how the layer engine work should be unblocked, chose "Accept the recommended starting values as provisional". So (a): shortest by travel time, counting each transfer; a flying unit ends on the layer it was ordered on; a pending hard transfer stays pending and a preferred layer is returned to where open. Each is a runtime setting, tuned by the skill lab. Folded into E-01 R-001-05. |
| Q-002-01 | E-01 / S-002 | Hard transfer from layer 1 to layer 5. Bible says units "transition between adjacent layers." Does the unit pass through 2, 3, 4 with a full transition time each, or make one transition of one duration? | (a) one transition of one duration regardless of distance; (b) one transition per step, duration × steps, unit exists on each intermediate layer in turn; (c) one transition, duration × steps, unit stays on the old layer until the end | closed | (a). "This is an implementation decision, and thus not mine, the expectation for functionality is solely from the user perspective, it should be 'reasonable' how quickly a unit traverses from one layer to another, between 1 and 2 seconds is a good starting point, to be tuned if needed." One transition, 1–2 seconds total. Answered by Westley, 2026-09-12. |
| Q-002-02 | E-01 / S-002 | A hard transfer is pending because the target layer is blocked here. How long does it stay pending? | (a) forever, until fulfilled or replaced; (b) until the unit's current move order completes; (c) until the unit is next idle | closed | (b), "until move order completes." Answered by Westley, 2026-09-13. |
| Q-003-01 | E-01 / S-003 | The preference modifier key default and the clear-preference input are both "TBD" in the bible. What are they? | (a) modifier Alt, clear is Alt+0; (b) modifier Ctrl, clear is Ctrl+0; (c) a button on the layer strip for clear, modifier Alt | closed | (a), modifier Alt + 1..5 for layer preference, Alt + 0 to clear. Answered by Westley, 2026-09-16. |
| Q-003-02 | E-01 / S-003 (R-003-07) | The layer-preference assignment (hold a modifier, press 1 to 5: the group prefers that layer when it moves on its own) names no modifier; Alt is the layer keys (transfer), Ctrl the control groups. | (a) Shift plus the number is the preference; (b) the preference assignment is dropped from the beta and R-003-07 retired | closed | Take Shift (Westley, 2026-10-13: "shift is still free", E-01:57). Architect, 2026-10-19 (implementation; Westley may veto); recorded 2026-10-21 from triage_2026-10-19.md. Earlier note: Westley 2026-10-13: "shift is still free". Default none; nothing is built on it. |
| Q-004-01 | E-01 / S-004 | Confirm the per-type transition durations as design values, not just tuning residue. Current config: Raider 1.0 s, Interceptor 1.2 s, Vanguard 2.5 s, Warden 2.0 s, Bombard 4.0 s, Bastion 5.0 s. Note Warden (Line) is faster than Vanguard (Line), which the "Strike fastest, Siege slowest" rule allows but does not explain. | (a) adopt as-is; (b) adopt with Warden and Vanguard both at 2.5 s; (c) other numbers | closed | (a), "as is." Answered by Westley, 2026-09-13. |
| Q-004-02 | E-01 / S-004 | During the transition window the bible says the unit cannot attack or be targeted by units on its old layer. Can it keep moving along its path? Can units on the new layer target it before it arrives? Can it be seen from either layer? | (a) keeps moving, untargetable by both layers, visible from both; (b) stops in place, untargetable by old layer only, targetable by new layer from the halfway point; (c) keeps moving, targetable by new layer from the start, visible from both | closed | New rule, not a lettered option. "It should still be attackable by those units until the transition is halfway completed. New layer can attack it after it's halfway completed. Visibility depends on the viewing unit's layer visibility [i.e. the cone/pulse cross-layer detection rules, not a blanket visible/invisible]." Old layer can attack until halfway; new layer can attack from halfway on; visibility follows the normal cross-layer sensor rules rather than an unconditional yes/no. Answered by Westley, 2026-09-13. |
| Q-004-03 | E-01 / S-004, S-001 | A unit walks up or down a slope that joins two layers. What measures the transition and its halfway point: the unit type's transition time, as everywhere else, or the slope itself? Raised 2026-09-30 when Westley asked for slopes in the layer lab, verbatim: "I'd like to modify it to include slopes, since that's important, there are rules about slopes documented in the bible (halfway is a cross-over)." The bible had no slope rule; the halfway rule he means is Q-004-02. | (a) timed by unit type: Raider 1.0 s, Bastion 5.0 s whatever the slope; halfway is a moment, and a transition once started completes; (b) set by the slope: progress along the slope is the transition, the time is slope length divided by unit speed (a 600-unit slope: Raider 4 s, Bastion 15 s), halfway is the slope's midline, a line on the map, and a unit may stop or turn back; the timed rule remains for a transfer made where there is no slope | closed | (b). Answered by Westley, 2026-09-30, verbatim: "Choice 2 set by the slope, no need for a switch." On a slope the slope sets the transition; the per-type timed rule stays for transfers away from slopes. No timed-versus-slope switch is built, in the game or in the layer lab. Folded into E-01 R-001-06, R-004-01, R-004-02, R-004-06 to R-004-08; what the answer leaves open is Q-004-04. |
| Q-004-04 | E-01 / S-004, S-001 | The slope ruling (Q-004-03) leaves four things unsaid: (1) where the ground is solid between two layers (a land era), may a unit change layer anywhere the target layer is open, or only by a slope; (2) may one slope join layers that are not adjacent; (3) R-004-02 says a transitioning unit cannot attack: does that hold for a unit on a slope (a Bastion on a 600-unit slope would be silent for 15 s); (4) which layer a unit on a slope counts as being on for its own sensing. | (a) [recommended] (1) only by a slope where the ground is solid; timed transfers only where both layers are open at that position, as in open space; (2) a slope joins two adjacent layers, and a taller climb is slopes in series, each with its own midline; (3) a unit on a slope may attack exactly the units that may attack it: the lower layer's below the midline, the upper layer's above it; (4) the layer on its side of the midline; (b) (1) anywhere the target layer is open, slopes being one way among others; (2) one slope may span several layers, with a cross-over at each boundary; (3) it cannot attack while on a slope; (4) the layer it entered from, until it leaves the slope; (c) other | closed | Answered by Westley, 2026-09-30, verbatim: "1: depends on the type of unit, flying/swimming units can traverse whenever as long as there's no blocker. Walking units can only move to a layer with land on it, and obviously can't teleport up multiple layers. 2: non-adjacent layers are fine, that's expected in real life. 3: transitioning units can attack, but if their transition cuts off their vision to the attacker, then they can't attack anymore, depends on the vision situation. 4: layer on it's side of the midline. Record that" (1) Flying and swimming units change layer anywhere nothing blocks; walking units stand only where a layer has land and change layer only by walking a slope, never in place. (2) A slope may join layers that are not adjacent. (3) A transitioning unit may attack while it can see its target, and stops when the transition takes that sight away; read as covering every transition, timed or on a slope. (4) A unit on a slope counts as on the layer on its side of the midline. Folded into E-01 R-001-06, R-001-07, R-004-02, R-004-06, R-004-08 and the invariants; what the answer leaves open is Q-004-05. |
| Q-004-05 | E-01 / S-004, S-001, E-11 | Westley's answer to Q-004-04 sorts units into walking and flying or swimming, lets a slope span several layers, and puts a unit on a slope on the layer on its side of the midline. It does not say: (1) which unit types walk and which fly or swim, and whether that is set per type in the roster or per era in the era pack (the Settled Reach's six types included); (2) how a map says where a layer has land a walker can stand on, as distinct from a layer that is merely open; (3) on a slope that spans more than two layers, where the cross-overs are; (4) whether a unit in a timed transfer counts as on its new layer from the halfway point, as a unit past a slope's midline does, or stays on its old layer until the transfer completes (the standing invariant); (5) what a walking unit does when given a hard transfer or a layer preference. | (a) [recommended] (1) a movement kind per unit type in the era pack, every Settled Reach type flying; (2) a ground flag per layer per position beside the open or blocked mask; (3) one cross-over at each layer boundary, evenly spaced along the slope, so the unit counts as on each layer in turn; (4) on the new layer from the halfway point; (5) it paths to a slope that reaches that layer and walks it, and the order is rejected when no slope does; (b) (1) a roster stat fixed across eras; (2) every open position has land; (3) a single midline between the bottom and top layers only; (4) on the old layer until the transfer completes; (5) the order is always rejected for a walking unit; (c) other | closed | (a), all five. Answered by Westley, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "9: looks good" and, on the layer of a unit in any transition, "10: I'm confused about this one, I thought the differentiator was *location* not *timing*. A unit can attack on the layer it's leaving until it hits the cross-over point, then it swaps to the layer it's moving to." Every transition has a cross-over point, halfway through a timed transfer and the midline of a slope: before it the unit is on the layer it is leaving, for attacking, being attacked and sensing; after it, on the layer it is moving to. Folded into E-01 R-001-06, R-001-07, R-004-02, R-004-06 and the invariants. |
| Q-005-01 | E-01 / S-005 | What counts as "at a vantage node" for the vantage doubling? Nodes are points with a position. | (a) within a fixed radius of the node position, proposed 300 wu; (b) within the node's authored zone radius from the layout; (c) inside the elevation gradient above a threshold, proposed 0.5 | closed | (a), pin/defer vantage doubling for prototype; all units use standard cross-layer cones. Answered by Westley, 2026-09-16. |
| Q-005-02 | E-01 / S-005 | Is cross-layer vision symmetric by layer distance? Production (`csrc/fog.c:585`, `om_unit_get_cross_layer_factor`) uses the absolute offset: 0.50 at one layer, 0.20 at two, in either direction. E-01 R-005-04 gives an enemy two layers above at 0.20, while R-005-05 says an enemy on layer 1 "(two layers below)" is never visible. | (a) [recommended] symmetric by absolute offset as production does; R-005-05 is reworded to say three or more layers away, in either direction; (b) asymmetric: looking down two layers sees nothing, looking up two sees at 0.20; (c) decided with Q-008-02 by the layer lab, which takes vision by signed offset so both can be tested | closed | (a), symmetric to start. Answered by Westley, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "7: let's start with symmetric but mark in the bible that going asymmetic could be a good idea if there's a "counter" to the highest layer being hte "best", say a unit that works particularly well at destroying units from above it to counter." Folded into E-01 R-005-05 and the note under S-005. Westley, 2026-10-05: "Q-005-02 this is still true, it's just that we're not seperating space from ground eras": the symmetric rule and its condition are the space model; ground eras use their own model (E-01 R-005-09 to R-005-12). |
| Q-005-03 | E-01 / S-005, S-006, S-008 | The layer system needs an action from the player, or it is static: with the same compositions, constant vision and equal range, two forces simply fire at each other as soon as they see each other. Westley, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "I'm thinking about the layers, and there's a flaw. If both units have the "same" compositions and the vision is constant, both units can just fire back at each other as soon as they see each other if they have the same range. And units with higher range will hypothetically have an advantage but they should be weaker to certain things, thus making the advantage temporary. I think we need to shift our methodology with regards to the layer system. I think that the layer system needs to be *utilized* somehow, not just static. I think we should create like a "rader pinging" system, and players have to actively ping specific layers to see vision on that layer. For eras that don't have technology, we could have them send out little scouts that cost nothing. The player would need to select specific units, select a "ping" ability, press a layer number and click where they want it to go, they'd then recieve a short burst of vision from that region for a very short time. Then the tuning would just become making sure that the *lines cross* when a player effectively uses the pings and swamps a smaller force. - Otherwise your list looks good, but I have a feeling that the layer system won't be distinctive without some specific action from the player. Research similar systems, come back with what other games/systems do, and come up with 2-3 other options other than radar for some way that players can utilize the system to introduce strategic depth." What is the action by which a player uses the layers? | (a) Westley's sketch, a targeted ping: selected units cast a ping at a chosen layer and place and get a very short burst of vision there; eras without the technology could send small free scouts (a proposal, open in Q-005-04); this differs from the sensor pulse of S-006, which is centred on the unit and reveals every layer; (b) going dark: each unit or group chooses to emit or stay silent; an emitting unit sees other layers at full range and is itself seen from every layer at long range, a silent one sees only its own layer and is hard to see from others (precedent: active against passive sonar, Nebulous, HighFleet, Starsector); (c) layer pickets: the player places cheap, persistent sensors on a layer to watch a region, and the enemy hunts and removes them, so vision is a contested resource (precedent: wards in Dota 2 and League of Legends; the research found persistent scouting is preferred to short paid reveals); (d) reach by unit type: each unit type can fire only at certain layer offsets (some only on their own layer, some up, some down), with a few types that cross or reach layers as counters, so the action is composing and placing types by layer (precedent: Supreme Commander and Beyond All Reason; Westley's own idea of a unit that is good at destroying units above it); (e) a combination or other. Research: `docs/legacy/topics/active_layer_vision_mechanics.md` | closed | (a), (b) and (d) together: the targeted ping, going dark, and reach by unit type. Answered by Westley, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "I think we should give players all 3: going dark, reach by unit type, and the pinging. To avoid the dislike for the short scan for money, these options will be free, we want to induce strategic depth. - units will still see others passively, just a shorter range, the pinging can have a much longer range. I'd also like the player being pinged to get an alert that they were pinged, and a ~60 degree direction that they were pinged from (like the direction indicator skill from World of Warships)." All three cost the player no resource. Passive cross-layer vision stays, at a shorter range; the ping reaches much further. A pinged player is alerted and shown a sector of about 60 degrees toward where the ping came from. Layer pickets (c) were not chosen. Folded into E-01 S-162, S-163, S-164 and S-008; the numbers and edges are Q-005-04. |

Closed from the record by the triage of 2026-10-19 (`plans/decision_pack_2026-10-18/triage_2026-10-19.md`), recorded 2026-10-21 (records clerk); cite: this row's own answer below (Westley 2026-10-01: "rough sizes only, not pass marks"). Fight end ruled, Westley 2026-10-13: "have both sets of units attack moved on the same location so they either move and stop or attack each other, unless there's a bug it's not possible for a tie (eventually). If there's one side that's required to hold a choke or a position, we could just make sure that the other side's attack move is close enough within vision/firing range that there will always be a victor, no units will be left standing around." So: a lab fight ends when one side is eliminated; both sides attack-move to one point, or the attacker's attack-move point is within the defender's vision and fire; the hold timer, hold radius, blind window and draw band are retired as end rules; a tick cap stays only as a bug detector and a capped fight is a lab defect to trace, never a result. Lab-side history: Rev 2 of the skill lab added its own DRAFT numbers, none of them Westley's yet (DRAFT-opening-window-ticks 40, REENGAGE_SILENCE_TICKS 40, the roster mixes M0 to M4 and M3b, the scale marks, GUARD_RADIUS 200 wu, REENGAGE_MOVED_WU 1 wu, COARSE_STEP 8, CONTROL_MAX_TICKS 600, and the arc and cone drafts of Q-100-01 and Q-082-06; the firing arc is Westley's 30 degrees, 2026-10-19); they are listed in README section 12 of the skill-lab-rev2-spec.

| Q-159-01 | E-19 / S-159 | Design for the player-run economy role in the deferred paired command-and-economy multiplayer mode. Westley wants the economy role's system complexity at roughly two to three times a proficient StarCraft 2 economy's, with building placement, city customization, and construction detail a major point (bigger, deeper city-building than Age of Empires Online), and no other constraint on mechanism. | (a) StarCraft 2-style worker, production, and supply management scaled up; (b) Age of Empires 2/Online-style multi-resource gathering and large-scale city building; (c) a bespoke system built for this game; (d) some combination, decided after prototyping | closed | (d), a combination built on a classic base. Answered by Westley across 2026-10-01; his words are verbatim in `docs/northstar/research/economy_design/README.md` and the system is in E-19 S-159 R-159-01 to R-159-13: classic Age of Empires Online and Age of Empires 3 as the base (three resources, literal workers, economic buildings, placement that matters), one population cap for workers and soldiers upgradeable to thousands, the economy player as production manager and technology chooser, expansions, haul distance, blind shipments, moving regional weather, counters and retooling, a production schedule with disguise, walls and raid lanes, forward bases. What remains open moved to Q-159-08 to Q-159-11. From 2026-10-02 these rules live in E-22 Economy and Bases (S-167 to S-182); the E-19 R-159 numbers above, other than R-159-01, are retired. |
| Q-159-02 | E-19 / S-159 | How the same economy and its city run themselves, with no real decision-making agent, in every match where nobody has opted into the paired mode (including the core single-commander game), and how they render cheaply enough to keep the screen visually busy at scale. Westley wants this to be a visual draw players are pulled toward and, ideally, can influence and grow even alone, not a passive backdrop; core-game economy management stays off (hub "The Player's Toolkit"). | (a) a scripted growth curve driven by the region's existing economic field (E-02), animating a procedural building-placement system akin to E-17 S-119's authored-pieces approach; (b) a lightweight rule-based city simulation with its own state, not just a curve; (c) other, decided after prototyping | closed | Answered by Westley, 2026-10-02, verbatim: "A3, player can change it on the fly in order to adapt to what the enemy is doing. B3 C2+4 D2+3, player can tune these by turning off one or the other. E1+3, player pick." Named doctrines the player can change on the fly; perfect hands and no foresight; one dial and asking by pointing; the rules pick units and counter what has been seen, each switchable; the enemy runs on the same rules and the player chooses whether it also scouts, counters and disguises (E-19 R-159-18 to R-159-21). How the self-built city is drawn is E-17 S-166. From 2026-10-02 these rules live in E-22 Economy and Bases (S-167 to S-182); the E-19 R-159 numbers above, other than R-159-01, are retired. |

| Q-008-02 | E-01 / S-008 | Cross-layer damage and vision are undefined and are the game's most important mechanic. Westley, 2026-09-23, verbatim: "the layer system dynamics are not fully defined, just an idea at this point. How units interact with each other cross-layer in terms of damage and vision are not yet defined, and that's probably the most important/unique part of this game. Let's make hashing that out a priority, I believe that prototypes are in order, comprehensive analysis of what configuration of damage/vision/landscape would induce the highest skill ceiling. Maybe we could take the "surround" idea into 3D, and define our own "Art of War" for 3 dimensional battles. Let's draw from high altitude ariel battle research. This section of the game deserves some real thought and experimentation, but I don't know how to test it without humans in the loop. We'd have to map out with research what makes games of the past have a high skill ceiling." | (a) a Track R research program (research queue Q76): what gave past games a high skill ceiling, aerial and high-altitude combat doctrine, a 3D surround theory, then prototypes with humans in the loop [recommended]; (b) design by analysis alone; (c) other | open | A research program by simulator, specified by Westley (a method of his own, neither option as lettered). It began as a standalone toy (`toys/layerlab/`, frozen 2026-10-02) and moved to the real simulation: the skill lab, record at `docs/northstar/research/layer_dynamics/README.md`, harness at `scripts/skilllab/`. Westley, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "yes to the real engine, so long as we can accomplish the original goal from the original plan." Westley, 2026-09-30, verbatim: "I'd like to start ( idea about layer simulation, simple prototype to tune numbers to produce strategy situations ) a specific method of figuring out how we'll do this. We will build a "toy" prototype. It will have the basic functions of the game, and be very fast, all C, bare minimum visuals maybe with python game library or something simple. It will contain the restraints of the system, layers, damage, vision, landscape. The prototype will run "simulations" of unit complex combinations: position, orientation, damage, health, anything that we can vary in an RTS. We'll use this simulator to document the relationships between all the variables and find the places that the "lines cross". We'll use this as a map for tuning units across different eras, and maintaining strategic depth. For example, we may notice that changing the vision variable only produces only strategic depth at a specific "value" given the values of all the other variables, however, if we change another one, the "lines cross" in more than one place, and induce strategic depth from two configurations of variables instead of one. This will require significant iterations and tuning, so the algorithms need to be fast. This is just an idea in my head currently, I want to explore it further and verify it works with a simple prototype before we commit to building the full thing." And: "Develop a plan to document the idea in the bible (with a pass after it's documented cross-referencing my original prompt with what was actually written for missed information or hallucinations), map out the dimensions, build a toy prototype of it, and find out if the hypothetical "lines crossing" is evident in the data (configurations that produce victory from the expected player given the conditions of the engagement). We don't need to do a large batch of thousands of "runs" and then analyze the data, I think it'd be much faster to iteratively try small things, check the output, and tune repetatively." On comparing the levers, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "1: I'd like to see what engagement looks like for all 3 of these options, let's build the lab to run simulations with all 3 of these knobs "turned"" (the three: vision only; vision and reach; those plus a damage penalty for firing up). On how expected winners are set, 2026-10-01, verbatim: "3: a but it's not just opening firing advantage, it's consistent firing advantage (the number that we really care about is number of units firing, if A and B both have the same number of units firing than the engagement ends up even)" On what strategic depth is, 2026-09-30, verbatim: "We'll need to clearly define what "strategic depth" looks like, but we can grab from other similar high skill games like Starcraft 2. For example, in starcraft 2 when units have a layer advantage, they can shoot without being shot back. When units have a surround, the units in the concave with the surround win. Organizing your units in specific patterns can lead to better engagements, depending on the unit, it's vulnerabilities and abilities, etc. For example: templar are very weak and slow and expensive, but when they use storm they're devestating to low-health units. A player that effectively protects them has a higher chance of winning. We will need to map all these dimensions out before we build anything." The target was restated on 2026-10-01, verbatim: "It's something along the lines of "players with better understanding of the landscape, unit formation, layer awareness/intentionality, and the other players current position can defeat a much larger force". The goal is to create a game with significant strategic depth. We get to this goal by tuning all the variables we laid out such that that sentence becomes a reality." Its conditions, of which the restatement around units firing was confirmed the same day, are in the record's section 4. Stays open until the lab yields numbers that fold into S-008 rules; the numbers themselves are Q-008-03. |

This one is pretty decided (we'll make the cards cool so the players have a desire to unlock them), no need for a second pass on this decision." The 2026-09-22 answer ("If it dies, the battle is over, re-enforcements can't come and you can't replenish your forces") was given for a city; Q-159-06 made the base not always a city. | (a) the battle ends for that side; (b) reinforcement and replenishment stop and the battle continues with what is on the field; (c) depends on the mission archetype | closed | Answered by Westley, 2026-10-02, verbatim: "A3+5, but at the point they've lost their original main base it's partial failure at maximum. A base is occupied, not destroyed, and can be retaken; a side falls back to a held expansion or forward base; losing the original main base caps the result at partial failure; archetype and supply distance together decide the kind of base (E-19 R-159-14, R-159-15). The same answer set resources and strongpoints (R-159-16, R-159-17). From 2026-10-02 these rules live in E-22 Economy and Bases (S-167 to S-182); the E-19 R-159 numbers above, other than R-159-01, are retired. |
| Q-166-01 | E-17 / S-166 | How do settlements build themselves, where do bases sit, and how does terrain height become layers? Six parts, each with options in `docs/northstar/research/economy_design/settlement_generation_options.md`: the density field, how the city reshapes around placed buildings, growth over time, the site of a base, terrain height and the five layers, and the scale of the canvas. | Westley picks per part | closed | Parts 1 to 5 answered by Westley, 2026-10-02: 1C with the correction that the engine never gives up at difficult terrain, 2A, 3C with 3A, 4B, 5B with 5C (E-17 R-166-03). Part 6 answered 2026-10-02: 6A, height bands are the layers, with a mix of benched and natural ground and some ground too steep to traverse (E-17 R-166-04). Part 7 answered 2026-10-02: 7D, one world with continuous detail; canvas stays at today's sizes for now with landscape variety packed in; zoom stops at the battlefield's map view (E-17 R-166-05). Bands answered 2026-10-02, verbatim: "fitted, if a layer step is only a meadow slope than there's a problem with the landscape generation system." The bands are fitted to each map and the generator must give real relief (E-17 R-166-06). This amends the note under Q-E01-01 that the elevation mapping is fixed globally; the count of five layers on every map stands. |
| Q-096-01 | E-13 / S-096, E-01 S-008 | A guard stance. Westley, 2026-10-01: "Yes a guard stance is fine". The ruled movement (R-087-04: an attack-moving unit chases a seen enemy and keeps chasing) defeats the anchor and leash as a guard: defenders at a choke run out through it, which is what spoiled the choke in the toy (history, pass 7). R-096-06 rejects a hold command. | (a) a guard order: the unit stays at its post, steps up to 200 wu from it to engage what its side sees, and returns when the target dies or leaves; engine and lab first, client later; R-096-06 and "four commands" reworded; (b) no new order: defenders are placed idle and never step forward; (c) other | closed | Westley, 2026-10-13: "B, record this as a decision in the whole repo for the whole game, not just the simulator". A radius guard: engages what its side sees within `GUARD_RADIUS` (draft 200 wu, DRAFT-guard-radius) of its post, returns when the target dies or leaves the radius, never chases past it; rule R-096-08 and S-208. The engine piece (step-and-return) is pending; the built guard holds and never steps (A170). |
| Q-097-02 | E-13 / S-097 | Two units on exactly the same spot. Westley, 2026-10-02, on collision: "the way that it worked on the Python simulation was perfect, and I'd like to migrate that way to be the way that it is in C as well." The Python moves the lower id in the -x direction and by a thousand times the normal push (the push is not normalised; port audit G14), which flings the unit; R-097-07 and the C move it +x by the normal push. | (a) [recommended] port the Python's collision except this case: keep the normal-sized push; direction as the Python (-x); (b) port it exactly, fling included; (c) keep R-097-07 as written (+x) | closed | Invisible tie-break. The fling was refused on 2026-10-03 (rulings-2:788, A173). Architect, 2026-10-19 (implementation; Westley may veto); recorded 2026-10-21 from triage_2026-10-19.md. |
| Q-097-03 | E-13 / S-097 | Obstacle repulsion (R-097-09) has no magnitude. The rule says units are pushed away from obstacles but gives no strength or range, and the Python's repulsion is a direction no-op (port audit G08: at density 1.0 the steer vector is zero). A full opposing vector would zero the move. The builder did not apply it to the movement step; units never enter a blocked cell, and a unit found inside one is moved to the nearest open cell. | (a) leave repulsion unbuilt: blocked cells alone keep units out; (b) a repulsion strength proportional to density within a stated range, both numbers Westley's; (c) the Python's no-op, documented. | closed | (a) Leave it unbuilt. Blocked cells already keep units out (rulings-2:711, A147). Architect, 2026-10-19 (implementation; Westley may veto); recorded 2026-10-21 from triage_2026-10-19.md. Earlier note: Opened 2026-10-02 by the movement builder (Architect A147). Unbuilt until it has a magnitude. |

The principle (units able to fire) does not cover this. | (a) it joins the principle: what counts is units firing and how that fire is concentrated; (b) it is a property to tune out, so the result follows the firing count alone; (c) decide from the focus-fire batch | closed | (c), done: nearest target stays the game's rule; the lab-only focus-fire setting was added and measured (a small real effect, under half a unit in 16). Closed from the record by the triage of 2026-10-19 (`plans/decision_pack_2026-10-18/triage_2026-10-19.md`), recorded 2026-10-21 (records clerk); cite: `plans/2026-10-01-architect-rulings-2.md` A196 and A200. Closed from the record by the triage of 2026-10-19 (`plans/decision_pack_2026-10-18/triage_2026-10-19.md`), recorded 2026-10-21 (records clerk); cite: Westley 2026-09-30: "not just unit values but their type as well" (candidate (a) above). | Q-166-02 | E-17 | How does the landscape engine work in space eras? Westley, 2026-10-02: "it's going to be a sister system, same generative style just different objects/outcome. Asteroid fields, open sections, places where you can cross over from upper to lower layers like an ice lake, this needs more design work so we can't write stories for it." Height bands, ground on all five layers, slopes and strongpoints (E-17 S-166) are written for land. | To be designed after the 2026-10-02 story round | closed | Direction from Westley, 2026-10-02: each layer is an open-or-blocked map, like sheets of ice over open water, with asteroids filling the blocked parts; heavy fields have tunnels, light fields are mostly open. First investigation and his words verbatim: `docs/northstar/research/space_landscape/README.md`. Design continues there.  Decided 2026-10-03: the functional map's minimum passage is 300 wu; a small unit class passes narrower gaps; small-craft gaps cover 5 to 10 percent of the landscape in batches, with the fine fracture kept there (README, "Decisions (2026-10-03): passage widths"). Open inside this: the small craft's size and gap width, and how the batches are chosen. Closed 2026-10-07: the approved space kit is the design. Westley, 2026-10-07, verbatim: "#2: a". Rules in E-17 S-246. |

Westley: "what tech will do exactly is undecided, mark that as something that needs designing." | To be designed | open | |

| Q-159-14 | E-22, E-10 | What is "supply distance"? The term came from an option Westley picked ("B1+2") and he does not recognise it. Offered meaning: how many regions lie between the mission's region and the nearest region the civilization owns, which sets whether a base generates on site, is supplied, or both. | (a) region hops from the nearest owned region; (b) drop the term, the archetype alone decides the base; (c) other | closed | Answered by Westley, 2026-10-02, verbatim: "1: keep". Supply distance is the number of regions between the mission's region and the nearest region the civilization owns; with the archetype it decides the kind of base (E-22 S-177, E-10 S-210, E-03 S-223). The thresholds in regions are a flag in those stories. |

| Q-232-01 | E-16 / S-232 | Keys and controls for the ping (a layer number and a click) and for switching a force between emitting and silent; also whether the ping and the layer-narrowing hotkey (R-105-06) share the number keys. | (a) decided with Q-103-04's camera keys; (b) ping on a held key plus 1 to 5, emit on a toggle key; (c) other | closed | Other. Westley, 2026-10-07, verbatim: "#7: every unit will have the same ping key (ability), remember we're using Immortal's style of all units abilities being on the same command card (no matter what the content of the group is, every ability they have access to is available to them on the card with a keystroke). We don't have to worry about abilities for the beta, but the ping is a cross-unit ability and will be on the same keystroke for every unit they select within a sub-group or when the whole group is selected. If they have a group selected, and use the ping ability, the unit closest to the ping location they click casts the ping (unless it's out of range, in which case they'll just need to move their cursor closer until it's in range." How the layer is chosen and the emit-or-silent key are Q-232-03. |

Then when the cursor switches back to regular when they get back in range that's a sufficient flag." The alert for the pinged side stays off (E-16 R-232-04, Westley, 2026-10-05). |

| Q-191-04 | E-17 / S-191 | On a terrain-first map, what does the archetype decide and what does the terrain decide? Q-119-02 closing removes the library that carried the archetype's shape. | Part 1, how they combine: (a) layout first, terrain obeys: terrain is forced at each node (a chokepoint gets a real pass, a vantage a real peak); (b) layout proposes, terrain disposes: the layout gives roles and rough zones, terrain is generated with a mild bias toward each zone's role, each node snaps to the best matching ground within a set radius, retry with another jitter a bounded number of times if none qualifies, moving objectives follow walking routes on the terrain; (c) terrain first, layout fitted: library layouts are scored against the terrain and the best fit is chosen. Part 2, what the archetype decides: (a) the archetype fixes canvas category, objective type and the sides' roles, the terrain the rest; (b) the archetype also biases the landform; (c) the archetype biases the site-finding weights | closed | Part 1 (b), part 2 (b). Westley, 2026-10-03, verbatim: "Q191-04: P1b, P2b." The layout library stays; the layout proposes and the terrain disposes, and the archetype also biases the landform (E-17 R-191-10). Reframed 2026-10-02 by Westley's answer under Q-119-02: the layout library stays and the terrain engine accounts for the layouts. The snap radius, the retry bound and the landform bias per archetype are Q-191-05. AMENDED 2026-10-03 by Westley, verbatim (under Q192-02): "This reminded me there's an issue with a decision I made in the last pass. I said that the layout engine only influences the landscape engine, but that ties into this question. The point of the game is that it induces strategic depth, not just random stochiacity. I want to modify my decision, the landscape engine should take the inputs from the layout engine and lean on them. For example, if the layout engine calls for something like a choke, the landscape engine is required to produce it. Mark that I want to see a prototype of the layout engine, and how the landscape engine reacts to it." Part 1 therefore becomes (a), the layout first and the terrain obeying: the landscape engine is required to produce what the layout calls for, and the soft snap and retry of (b) go. Part 2 (b) stands. |

| Q-192-03 | E-17 / S-192, S-197 | A vantage is a peak, so it sits on an upper layer (4 or 5). On a ground era, must walking units be able to get up to it? (R-113-04 requires this today; E-17 R-197-03 requires every vantage reachable by both sides.) | (a) yes, by slope (implied by Q-189-01 for ground eras); (b) no, flying units only; (c) at least one vantage per map is walkable | closed | Westley asked, 2026-10-03, verbatim: "Q192-03: what is a high band?" Answer to him: the five layers are the height bands fitted to each map (E-17 R-188-01), layer 1 lowest, layer 5 highest; "high band" was shorthand for the upper layers, where peaks are (the layouts mark vantage zones `elevation_hint: "high"`). Your answer to Q-189-01 (a walking route to every band on a ground era) already implies (a) for ground eras. Waiting for his yes or no. ANSWERED 2026-10-03, Westley, verbatim: "Q192-03: a most of the time, it's ok if ~30% percent of peaks are unreachable, that's natural". Recorded as (a) with a tolerance: a vantage is walkable by slope on a ground era, and about 30 percent of peaks may be unreachable on foot (E-17 R-192-03). NOTE 2026-10-21 (Westley, C10 "A", DEC-C10-2026-10-21): the 30 percent tolerance is narrowed to peaks that are not vantages; every vantage point and strategic point has at least two routes (E-17 R-189-07, R-192-03). |
| Q-192-04 | E-17 / S-192 | The layout engine decides what a map holds, and the landscape engine must produce it. The old layouts have chokes, vantages and flank routes but no node kind for a slope top or a tall-cover place, and layout 07 (Suppression and Counterinsurgency) has no choke or vantage. How does a map come to offer the plays of the layer system (hold a choke, hold the top of a slope past its midline, fight from behind tall cover), and are the places S-192 finds by rule added to the layout's, or only used where the layout names none of that kind? | (a) [recommended] layouts gain node kinds for slope tops and tall-cover places and each layout declares the plays it offers; the terrain adds chokes, vantages and strongpoints by rule only where the layout names none of that kind; a layout that offers no play is allowed; (b) layouts stay as they are and the terrain finds the plays by rule, counted over a run of maps (the layer lab's 80 percent condition); (c) report only | closed | Westley, 2026-10-03, verbatim: "Q192-04: This is dependent on the layout engine, which should be documented in the old bible but maybe not clearly. Investigate what's been decided on that front (I remember seeing pngs of layouts from a very old prototype)." Findings: the eight layouts are node graphs in `simulation/layout_library/layout_0N_*.json` (Python; ALG-70 to ALG-73), shown as dots on five layer panels in `simulation/tuning_output/stage7/checkpoint_e/` and `checkpoint_f/layout_0N_*.png` (no terrain drawn). Per layout: chokepoints 0 to 3 (layout 07, scattered outposts, has none), vantages 0 to 2 (none in 04 and 07), reinforcement-structure nodes 0 to 2, flank routes 0 to 4. The library has no node kind for a slope top or for tall cover, and no numeric choke width. What the layout guarantees today: at least one objective and staging node, a flank bypass for every choke, vantages reachable by both sides (R-113-02 to R-113-04), and at least 2 routes to each objective (R-112-04). Nothing says how many plays a map must offer. With the layout engine leading (Q-191-04 amendment), the question is restated. ANSWERED 2026-10-03, Westley, verbatim: "Q192-04: a, no. We should probably pair archetypes with layouts, the game has changed a lot since those layouts were authored. Mark that layouts need to be audited/double checked before they're implemented." Recorded as (a); and "no": a layout that offers no play is not allowed, so layout 07 (scattered outposts) must gain one or be replaced. The layout library is audited before it is implemented, and archetypes are probably paired with layouts (ROADMAP section 4). |

| Q-007-01 | E-01 / S-007 (R-007-07) | Does cover or ground between an observer and a target on different layers block the sight line or the line of fire, and how? E-01 R-007-01 and R-007-02 cover only units on the same layer, and on land maps the ground now fills layers, so ridges are most of the occlusion. | (a) the same rule as the same-layer case, with the line's height interpolated between the two layers; (b) ground blocks, cover blocks only up to its own height; (c) decided by the skill lab | closed | (a): the same rule as the same-layer case, with the line's height interpolated between the two layers. Blocked fire is handled like blocked movement: a unit in range with the line blocked walks around until it finds a firing spot and gives up for another target after 1 second (E-11 R-087-09), and the landscape engine keeps obstacles to a minimum width so an obstacle is a detour, not a stop. Westley, 2026-10-07, verbatim: "#1: A, we may need to include unit firing awareness just as units have spacial awareness, for example if a unit has an attack move order, but can't get close enough to be in range, it walks around other units blocking it until it finds a spot to shoot. This would be the case that it's in range, but blocked. It'd be smart if we could copy+paste the same algorithm for blocked movement into blocked firing. This means that objects that are in the way don't just completely *stop* units that are trying to attack an enemy, if we make sure the landscape engine keeps obstacles to a minimum width, this will lead to obstacles being a small annoyance instead of a complete blocker." and, later, "#1 yes a unit gives up to pick another target if it can't find a firing spot within 1 second." |

| Q-163-02 | E-01 / S-163 (R-163-03) | What does emitting cost? On 2026-10-05 the switch itself cost no resource (R-163-03); on 2026-10-07 Westley said emitting needs a cost, and that being seen from further must exceed the extra sight. | (a) the exposure alone is the cost, the seen-from distance growing by more than the sight does; (b) the exposure plus a running resource cost while emitting (Uranium or Metal in space); (c) the exposure plus a warm-up delay; (d) other | closed | (a): the exposure alone. "Cost" means a drawback, not a resource: emitting must not be free, or players would always emit. The drawback is an increased detectability radius from opponents, and nothing else (no resource, no delay). Westley, 2026-10-07, verbatim: "#14: When I said cost, I didn't mean literal resource cost, I mean drawback. So, emmitting with no drawback will lead to players just always emmitting, the point of there being a \"cost\" is to create a more dynamic situation. The \"cost\" is increased detectability radius from opponents, that's it." |
